Exam name: CERTIFIED TACTICAL PARAMEDIC
1. 
You are deployed with a regional SWAT team in a US metropolitan area. During a dynamic entry, an officer is shot in the femoral artery. The scene is still hot, and the suspect is actively firing from a barricaded position. What is your immediate medical priority?
A. 
Apply a tourniquet high and tight over the uniform
B. 
Extricate the officer to the cold zone immediately
C. 
Return fire and direct the casualty to apply self-aid if able
D. 
Administer 1 gram of TXA via auto-injector
2. 
A casualty has a gunshot wound to the right chest. They are experiencing progressive respiratory distress and decreasing oxygen saturation. After placing a vented chest seal, their condition worsens, and they display absent breath sounds on the right side. What is the next appropriate action?
A. 
Perform a needle decompression in the second intercostal space, mid-clavicular line
B. 
Remove the chest seal to allow trapped air to escape, then replace or reassess
C. 
Administer high-flow oxygen via non-rebreather mask
D. 
Initiate positive pressure ventilation with a bag-valve-mask
3. 
A joint task force is responding to a suspected clandestine laboratory in the Midwest. Upon entry, several team members experience pinpoint pupils, excessive salivation, and muscle twitching. Which pharmacological intervention is most critical for these initial symptoms?
A. 
Atropine 2 mg intramuscularly
B. 
Naloxone 2 mg intranasally
C. 
Pralidoxime chloride (2-PAM) 600 mg intramuscularly
D. 
Midazolam 5 mg intramuscularly
4. 
A US Marshals operational K9 sustains a deep laceration to the axillary region during a suspect apprehension. Direct pressure is failing, and the wound is not amenable to a standard tourniquet. What is the most appropriate next step for hemorrhage control?
A. 
Apply a standard windlass tourniquet proximal to the wound
B. 
Pack the wound with a hemostatic dressing and apply continuous pressure
C. 
Administer 10 mg/kg of TXA intravenously
D. 
Apply a pressure dressing using an elastic bandage and standard gauze
5. 
Following an IED detonation at a domestic checkpoint, a casualty presents with intact tympanic membranes but complains of diffuse abdominal pain and nausea. Vital signs show mild tachycardia. What is the primary concern regarding blast injury mechanisms for this patient?
A. 
Primary blast injury causing hollow organ barotrauma
B. 
Secondary blast injury from penetrating shrapnel
C. 
Tertiary blast injury from being thrown by the blast wave
D. 
Quaternary blast injury from associated thermal burns
6. 
An active shooter incident is occurring at a large commercial venue. As part of a Rescue Task Force (RTF), you are moving into the Warm Zone with a police escort. Your primary medical objective in this zone is to:
A. 
Establish a formal triage sector and extensive treatment area
B. 
Perform comprehensive secondary assessments on all victims
C. 
Execute rapid hemorrhage control and airway management, then evacuate
D. 
Initiate intravenous fluid resuscitation for all shock patients
7. 
A casualty with a documented history of severe traumatic brain injury and non-compressible truncal hemorrhage is being prepared for tactical evacuation. The injury occurred 45 minutes ago. Which is the correct protocol for Tranexamic Acid (TXA. administration under current TECC guidelines?
A. 
1 gram of TXA in 100 mL Normal Saline or Lactated Ringer's over 10 minutes
B. 
2 grams of TXA via slow IV push over 1 minute
C. 
2 grams of TXA via intraosseous push followed by a 20 mL flush
D. 
TXA is contraindicated due to the presence of a traumatic brain injury
8. 
You are providing medical support for a federal law enforcement raid. A suspect is wounded during the exchange and drops their weapon, surrendering. A wounded officer is 10 yards away. According to tactical medical ethics and standard rules of engagement, how should you prioritize care once the area is entirely secured?
A. 
The wounded officer must be treated and evacuated before the suspect is assessed
B. 
Medical care is rendered based on the severity of the injuries, regardless of the patient's status
C. 
The suspect is treated only after all law enforcement personnel have been medically cleared
D. 
Care for the suspect is delayed until local EMS arrives, preserving tactical resources for the team
9. 
A patient sustained a gunshot wound from a high-velocity rifle. The entrance wound is small, but the exit wound is massive with extensive tissue destruction. This wound profile is primarily due to:
A. 
The bullet tumbling and fragmenting as it transfers kinetic energy
B. 
The direct cutting action of the bullet's leading edge
C. 
The heat generated by the bullet cauterizing the tissue
D. 
The low yaw angle maintained by the projectile throughout the tissue track
10. 
A tactical team is on a 48-hour tracking mission in a remote national forest. A team member develops signs of exertional heat stroke, presenting with altered mental status and a high core temperature. Ice water immersion is impossible. What is the most effective alternative cooling method available in an austere environment?
A. 
Administering antipyretic medications such as oral acetaminophen
B. 
Removing excess gear and applying continuous misting with aggressive fanning
C. 
Providing cold oral hydration fluids in large quantities
D. 
Applying chemical cold packs strictly to the forehead and chest
11. 
During the Tactical Evacuation phase via helicopter, a patient with a massive hemorrhage goes into cardiac arrest. Hemorrhage has been controlled, and the airway is secure. What is the most appropriate resuscitation strategy?
A. 
Initiate standard CPR and administer epinephrine 1 mg every 3-5 minutes
B. 
Perform bilateral needle decompression and initiate rapid administration of whole blood or fluid
C. 
Withhold resuscitative efforts as traumatic cardiac arrest in a tactical setting is universally fatal
D. 
Apply an automated external defibrillator (AED. and deliver a shock immediately
12. 
When developing a medical threat assessment for a planned VIP protection detail in Washington D.C., which factor should be prioritized to ensure effective tactical evacuation?
A. 
The specific manufacturer of the team's individual first aid kits
B. 
The identification of Level I trauma centers and routes, including traffic pattern analysis
C. 
The acquisition of advanced surgical equipment for the team vehicle
D. 
The historical crime statistics of the specific neighborhood over the last decade
13. 
A casualty has severe facial trauma with compromised airway reflexes. They are unconscious but breathing spontaneously. Attempts to place a nasopharyngeal airway are unsuccessful due to anatomical destruction. What is the preferred definitive airway intervention in the Tactical Field Care phase?
A. 
Endotracheal intubation using direct laryngoscopy
B. 
Surgical cricothyroidotomy
C. 
Placement of an extraglottic airway device (e.g., i-gel)
D. 
Positioning the patient in the recovery position and monitoring
14. 
A conscious casualty with an isolated, severe lower extremity fracture requires analgesia during a prolonged extrication. They have normal vital signs and no signs of shock. According to TECC guidelines, what is the preferred analgesic approach if intravenous access is not yet established?
A. 
Intramuscular administration of 10 mg of morphine sulfate
B. 
Oral transmucosal fentanyl citrate (OTFC. 800 mcg lozenge
C. 
Intravenous administration of 50 mcg of fentanyl
D. 
Oral administration of meloxicam and acetaminophen
15. 
A flashbang deployment in an enclosed space results in a team member sustaining partial-thickness burns to approximately 15% of their total body surface area. What is the primary focus of burn care during the Tactical Field Care phase?
A. 
Rapid initiation of the Parkland formula using Lactated Ringer's
B. 
Aggressive debridement of blisters to prevent infection
C. 
Application of wet dressings to cool the burns and provide comfort
D. 
Preventing hypothermia and covering the burns with dry, sterile dressings
16. 
To ensure maximum operational effectiveness, a tactical paramedic's integration with a law enforcement SWAT team requires:
A. 
Waiting at the command post until the scene is completely secured and cold
B. 
Cross-training in basic tactical movements and communication protocols
C. 
Carrying primary entry weapons and leading room clearing operations
D. 
Operating exclusively under the direction of local civilian EMS protocols
17. 
A casualty has sustained multiple gunshot wounds and is experiencing hemorrhagic shock. Environmental temperatures are moderate (70°F). Why is the active prevention of hypothermia critical in this patient?
A. 
Hypothermia directly induces a state of hypercoagulability
B. 
It prevents the exacerbation of the trauma triad of death (hypothermia, acidosis, coagulopathy)
C. 
Shivering increases the patient's metabolic demand, thereby improving oxygenation
D. 
Moderate temperatures eliminate the risk of clinically significant hypothermia
18. 
A casualty has a suspected pelvic fracture following a fall from a height during a vertical assault. They are hemodynamically unstable. Which intervention is most appropriate prior to evacuation?
A. 
Application of bilateral traction splints
B. 
Application of a commercial pelvic binder at the level of the greater trochanters
C. 
Application of a pneumatic anti-shock garment (PASG)
D. 
Securing the patient to a rigid long spine board without additional pelvic stabilization
19. 
A victim is trapped under concrete rubble following a structural collapse for 4 hours. Extrication is imminent. To mitigate the effects of crush syndrome upon release of the compressing weight, the medical provider should prepare to administer:
A. 
Intravenous calcium chloride and sodium bicarbonate
B. 
High-dose corticosteroids and antihistamines
C. 
Intramuscular epinephrine and albuterol nebulizers
D. 
Oral antibiotics and non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs
20. 
During an active violence incident at a school, you encounter a pediatric casualty with a massive hemorrhage from a leg wound. What is the guidance regarding the use of commercial windlass tourniquets on pediatric patients?
A. 
Commercial tourniquets are strictly contraindicated; manual pressure must be used
B. 
Only specialized pediatric elastic tourniquets should be applied
C. 
Adult commercial tourniquets can and should be used if they can be tightened sufficiently
D. 
Tourniquets should only be applied to the upper extremities in children
21. 
A tactical team is operating in a major US metropolitan transit hub when a domestic terror cell releases a suspected nerve agent. Several bystanders and two operators begin displaying symptoms of pinpoint pupils, respiratory distress, and severe muscle twitching. What is the immediate pharmacological priority in the Tactical Field Care phase?
A. 
Administering intravenous epinephrine and sodium bicarbonate
B. 
Rapid sequence intubation and immediate helicopter evacuation
C. 
Administration of atropine and pralidoxime chloride (2-PAM)
D. 
Aggressive fluid resuscitation with normal saline
22. 
A SWAT operator sustains severe maxillofacial trauma from a close-range blast but remains conscious and is actively resisting being laid flat on a backboard. They are breathing spontaneously but with difficulty. The tactical situation is stabilizing. What is the most appropriate initial airway management strategy?
A. 
Allowing the casualty to sit up and lean forward in a position of comfort
B. 
Forcing the casualty into a supine position to protect the cervical spine
C. 
Immediately initiating a surgical cricothyroidotomy
D. 
Inserting a nasopharyngeal airway blindly
23. 
During a barricaded suspect standoff, a casualty is struck in the thigh by a high-velocity 5.56mm rifle round. While the entrance wound is small, surgical exploration later reveals massive internal tissue destruction extending far beyond the actual path of the projectile. This specific phenomenon is primarily caused by:
A. 
The bullet's low yaw angle maintaining a narrow track
B. 
Temporary cavitation stretching the surrounding tissue beyond its elastic limit
C. 
The heat of the projectile cauterizing tissue upon entry
D. 
The direct cutting action of the bullet's leading edge
24. 
You are deployed as part of a Rescue Task Force (RTF) entering the warm zone of an active violence incident at a local high school. Triage must be rapid. According to standard TECC/Triage guidelines, which of the following casualties should receive a black tag (expectant)?
A. 
A casualty with a gunshot wound to the femur and absent distal pulses
B. 
A conscious casualty with an open, sucking chest wound
C. 
A casualty with a partial amputation of the hand who is screaming in pain
D. 
An unresponsive casualty with no palpable radial pulse who remains apneic after manual airway repositioning
25. 
A US Customs and Border Protection operational K9 sustains a deep, arterial laceration to its right hind leg from razor wire while tracking a suspect in an austere environment. The handler cannot stop the bleeding with direct pressure. Where should a commercial K9 tourniquet be applied?
A. 
High and tight on the affected limb, proximal to the injury
B. 
Directly over the wound site to compress the severed vessel
C. 
Two inches above the wound, avoiding joint spaces
D. 
Tourniquets are contraindicated in K9s; direct pressure must be maintained
26. 
A tactical medic is attached to a federal fugitive task force operating in severe winter conditions (15°F). A suspect goes into cardiac arrest following a high-speed vehicle rollover. The cardiac monitor shows ventricular fibrillation, and the patient's core temperature is estimated to be 84°F. What is the modified ACLS approach for this severely hypothermic patient in a tactical setting?
A. 
Administer epinephrine 1 mg IV push every 3-5 minutes immediately
B. 
Withhold all resuscitation efforts as traumatic arrest is universally fatal
C. 
Deliver a single defibrillation shock, resume CPR, and focus on active core rewarming
D. 
Proceed with the standard ACLS algorithm including multiple shocks and antiarrhythmics
27. 
Due to a sudden structural collapse during an urban search and rescue operation, a team member's lower extremities are pinned under concrete for 6 hours. As heavy equipment arrives to lift the debris, what is the critical medical intervention required immediately prior to and during extrication to prevent fatal crush syndrome complications?
A. 
Administration of oral loop diuretics to stimulate kidney function
B. 
Aggressive intravenous fluid therapy with normal saline to maintain urine output
C. 
Immediate fasciotomy of the affected extremities in the field
D. 
Application of bilateral commercial tourniquets prior to the release of the crushing weight
28. 
Your team is receiving effective, heavy suppressive fire from an elevated position. An operator ten feet away is struck in the arm and is bleeding profusely from a severed brachial artery. You are currently in the Care Under Fire phase. What is the only appropriate medical intervention at this exact moment?
A. 
Performing a rapid surgical cricothyroidotomy for airway protection
B. 
Administering Tranexamic Acid (TXA. for internal bleeding mitigation
C. 
Decompressing a suspected tension pneumothorax
D. 
Application of a limb tourniquet to control life-threatening hemorrhage
29. 
A DEA tactical team is preparing to breach a suspected large-scale illicit fentanyl milling operation. In preparing the medical threat assessment, what specific countermeasure must be prioritized to ensure operator safety upon entry?
A. 
Ensuring all personnel are issued Level A HAZMAT suits for the breach
B. 
Pre-positioning a mobile surgical unit at the immediate perimeter
C. 
Equipping all entry and medical personnel with readily accessible high-dose naloxone
D. 
Establishing an immediate air medical evacuation corridor exclusively for this operation
30. 
A casualty has sustained a partial amputation of the lower leg from an improvised explosive device. A tourniquet has been applied, and hemorrhage is controlled, but the patient remains in hypovolemic shock with a blood pressure of 80/50 mmHg. The patient is in severe pain. According to tactical analgesia protocols, which medication is most appropriate?
A. 
Oral transmucosal fentanyl citrate (OTFC. 800 mcg
B. 
Ketamine 50 mg intramuscularly or 20 mg intravenously/intraosseously
C. 
Morphine sulfate 10 mg intravenously
D. 
Meloxicam 15 mg and acetaminophen 1000 mg orally
31. 
During a protracted domestic hostage situation, a barricaded suspect demands that a tactical medic provide advanced medical supplies for a hostage they claim is injured, in exchange for delaying execution. What is the primary ethical and operational guideline for the tactical medic in this scenario?
A. 
Provide supplies only if explicitly authorized by the tactical commander, prioritizing team and operational security
B. 
Refuse to provide any medical supplies under any circumstances as it violates standard civilian EMS protocols
C. 
Independently approach the barricade under a flag of truce to deliver the supplies and assess the hostage
D. 
Instruct the suspect on how to perform self-aid using improvised materials over a loudspeaker
32. 
Following an accidental explosion at a local manufacturing plant, a SWAT operator who was near the blast zone presents with shortness of breath, a suddenly decreasing heart rate, and falling blood pressure. They have no visible shrapnel wounds or blunt trauma. These symptoms are most indicative of:
A. 
Blunt force trauma to the chest wall from being thrown
B. 
Penetrating injuries from flying glass and microscopic debris
C. 
Primary blast injury causing pulmonary barotrauma and potential air embolism
D. 
Thermal burns to the upper airway causing secondary swelling
33. 
You are managing a casualty with a severe Traumatic Brain Injury (TBI) who is being transported via a tactical helicopter flight lasting 45 minutes. To prevent secondary brain injury during the Tactical Evacuation phase, you must strictly focus on:
A. 
Inducing prophylactic hypothermia to reduce cerebral metabolic demand
B. 
Administering hypotonic intravenous fluids to maintain hydration
C. 
Encouraging hyperventilation to achieve a target EtCO2 of 25 mmHg
D. 
Maintaining oxygen saturation &amp;gt;90% and strictly avoiding hyperventilation
34. 
A casualty was involved in a high-speed vehicle pursuit that ended in a severe crash. They sustained blunt chest trauma from the steering wheel. During your secondary assessment, you note a classic presentation of cardiac tamponade. Which set of clinical signs (Beck's TriaD. confirms this suspicion?
A. 
Hypertension, bradycardia, and irregular respirations
B. 
Muffled heart tones, jugular venous distention, and narrowing pulse pressure
C. 
Tracheal deviation, absent breath sounds, and profound hypotension
D. 
Tachycardia, flat neck veins, and a widening pulse pressure
35. 
You have successfully performed a needle decompression on a casualty with a suspected tension pneumothorax. Their respiratory distress initially improved, but 15 minutes later during transport to the extraction zone, their oxygen saturation drops drastically, and they become highly distressed again. What is your immediate next step?
A. 
Perform a second needle decompression adjacent to the first insertion site
B. 
Remove the initial needle and insert a larger gauge catheter into the exact same hole
C. 
Immediately upgrade the intervention to a tube thoracostomy (chest tube)
D. 
Administer positive pressure ventilation forcefully with a bag-valve-mask
36. 
A massive, coordinated terrorist attack has occurred at a major US shopping mall, drawing responses from local police, FBI, state police, and multiple municipal EMS agencies. To ensure tactical medical resources are not mismanaged in the ensuing chaos, the medical response must immediately:
A. 
Operate independently based on internal agency Standard Operating Procedures to save time
B. 
Defer all medical decisions exclusively to the highest-ranking federal agent physically present
C. 
Integrate into a unified command structure utilizing NIMS and ICS protocols
D. 
Establish completely separate triage sectors and communication channels for each responding agency
37. 
A tactical team is attempting to clear a building where rioters have deployed stolen CS gas (a riot control agent). Several operators breach the perimeter and are immediately overcome by the gas, experiencing severe eye watering, burning skin, and coughing. The immediate tactical field care action for these operators is:
A. 
Administering a prophylactic dose of atropine intramuscularly to counteract the agent
B. 
Moving the team upwind, removing contaminated gear, and flushing eyes with copious amounts of water
C. 
Instructing the team to hold their breath, push through the pain, and continue the assault
D. 
Applying an occlusive dressing over the eyes and nose to prevent further vapor exposure
38.

B. 
Amoxicillin/clavulanate 875 mg orally
C.

D. 
Moxifloxacin 400 mg orally once a day
39. 
You are tasked with providing prolonged field care for a US Special Operations team in a highly austere environment where evacuation is delayed by 72 hours. A casualty requires a massive blood transfusion, but you have exhausted all stored blood products. What is the preferred method for field blood transfusion in this scenario?
A. 
Utilizing pre-screened low-titer O-negative or O-positive donors directly from the tactical team
B. 
Administering type-specific uncrossmatched blood from any willing local civilian bystander
C. 
Withholding any further transfusion until fully crossmatched blood arrives from a higher echelon of care
D. 
Administering hypertonic saline as a direct, equal-volume substitute for whole blood
40. 
An operator sustains a deep junctional hemorrhage to the groin after triggering a tripwire. The bleeding cannot be controlled with a standard windlass tourniquet. You proceed to pack the wound tightly with a combat gauze hemostatic dressing. To ensure the hemostatic agent is effective, you must:
A. 
Secure the dressing with a pressure bandage immediately and quickly move to the next patient
B. 
Remove the hemostatic dressing and replace it with standard cotton gauze if bleeding is still visible
C. 
Apply direct, continuous pressure for a minimum of 3 minutes over the packed hemostatic dressing
D. 
Elevate the affected limb above the level of the heart without applying any manual pressure to the wound
41.

Heart rate 40-60 bpm, Respiratory rate 8-15 breaths/min, Temperature 98.6°F

42.

43.

45.

47.

48.

C.

D.

49.

D.

50.

51. 
A tactical paramedic responds to a domestic terrorism incident. While treating a critically injured suspect in the back of an armored BearCat, a federal investigator demands to know the suspect's statements regarding the location of secondary devices. Under standard legal doctrines, does providing this information violate the patient's HIPAA privacy rights?
A. 
Yes, healthcare providers are bound by strict confidentiality regardless of the tactical situation
B. 
Yes, unless the investigator presents a signed warrant immediately on the scene
C. 
No, because suspects in federal custody forfeit all rights to medical privacy
D. 
No, the exigent circumstances and threat to public safety provide an explicit exception to HIPAA regulations
52. 
An operator takes a direct hit to their ballistic face shield from a high-caliber round, resulting in catastrophic failure of the shield and severe crush injury to the larynx and mandible. The patient is fighting for air, oxygen saturations are rapidly falling, and anatomical landmarks for the neck are highly distorted. What is the most appropriate airway intervention?

Performing a surgical cricothyroidotomy utilizing the "bougie-assisted" technique
B. 
Blind insertion of dual nasopharyngeal airways and high-flow oxygen
C.

D. 
Immediate insertion of an extraglottic airway device (e.g., King LT or i-gel)
53. 
A casualty had a hasty windlass tourniquet applied to their upper right thigh during the Care Under Fire phase for a gunshot wound. It has been 90 minutes since application. The patient is now in the Tactical Field Care phase, conscious, and hemodynamically stable. The tactical situation permits time for wound assessment. What is the correct protocol regarding tourniquet conversion in this scenario?
A. 
The tourniquet must be left in place until the casualty reaches a surgical facility, regardless of transport time
B. 
Loosen the tourniquet slowly over 5 minutes to assess for recurrent bleeding; if none, leave it loose but in place
C. 
Pack the wound with a hemostatic dressing, apply a pressure bandage, and gradually release the tourniquet while monitoring for bleeding
D. 
Immediately remove the tourniquet and replace it with a pressure point hold at the femoral artery
54. 
During a prolonged track in hot, humid conditions, an operational K9 collapses. The handler reports the dog was panting excessively, became uncoordinated, and is now unresponsive. You assess the core temperature via a rectal thermometer at 107°F. What is your immediate treatment priority for this K9?

C.

D.

55. 
You are the medical lead for a multi-agency response to a radiological dispersal device (dirty bomB. detonation. You must advise the incident commander on establishing a safe perimeter. According to the principles of radiation protection (Time, Distance, Shielding), how does doubling the distance from a point source of radiation affect the dose rate exposure?
A. 
It reduces the dose rate to one-half of the original exposure
B. 
It reduces the dose rate to one-third of the original exposure
C. 
It has no effect on the dose rate without additional shielding
D. 
It reduces the dose rate to one-fourth of the original exposure
56. 
Your Rescue Task Force (RTF) is operating in a warm zone of an office building. You encounter three casualties: Patient A has a penetrating chest wound and is struggling to breathe; Patient B has a gunshot wound to the calf with a previously applied, effective tourniquet; Patient C is pulseless, apneic, and has a massive head injury. You have two security personnel to assist with extrication. What is the most appropriate action?
A. 
Initiate CPR on Patient C, secure Patient A's chest wound, and tell Patient B to walk out
B. 
Perform needle decompression on Patient A, start an IV on Patient B, and bag-valve-mask Patient C
C.

D.

A tracheostomy completely severs the vocal cords, rendering the operator permanently mute, whereas a cricothyroidotomy is entirely reversible.
D. 
The cricothyroid membrane is superficial, easily palpable, and relatively avascular, making the procedure exponentially faster and safer to perform under fire than a tracheostomy, which risks massive hemorrhage from the highly vascular thyroid gland and inferior thyroid veins.
581. 
You are 14 hours into a prolonged field care scenario. A casualty with massive bilateral amputations is receiving their fourth unit of cold-stored whole blood. The cardiac monitor displays a progressively widening QRS complex and a prolonged QT interval, and the patient exhibits severe carpopedal spasms. You correctly identify citrate toxicity (iatrogenic hypocalcemiA. and prepare to administer Calcium Gluconate. What critical vascular access protocol must be strictly observed during this administration?
A. 
The Calcium must be administered via a separate, dedicated intravenous line, or the existing line must be flushed aggressively with saline, as Calcium will fatally precipitate and clot when mixed directly with the citrate in the blood products.
B. 
The Calcium must be forcefully mixed directly into the whole blood bag prior to infusion to chemically pre-neutralize the citrate.
C. 
The Calcium must only be administered via the intraosseous (IO) route, as peripheral intravenous administration during hemorrhagic shock causes instant tissue necrosis.
D. 
The Calcium must be administered concurrently with a high-dose infusion of Heparin to prevent the newly formed calcium from triggering systemic micro-emboli.
582.

D.

584. 
During a high-altitude tactical evacuation in an unpressurized fixed-wing aircraft, a casualty with severe hemorrhagic shock is receiving a continuous intravenous drip of Tranexamic Acid (TXA. running via a gravity-fed IV bag. As the aircraft rapidly ascends from sea level to 15,000 feet to clear a mountain range, what potentially catastrophic iatrogenic complication must the flight medic actively anticipate regarding the IV setup?
A.

B. 
The extreme cold at altitude will cause the TXA to crystallize inside the IV catheter, completely occluding the line.
C.

D. 
The decreased partial pressure of oxygen at altitude causes the TXA to chemically degrade into a highly toxic, vasodepressive analogue.
585. 
An operator has been trapped beneath a collapsed structural beam for 16 hours. What is the specific, dual-purpose cellular mechanism of the Sodium Bicarbonate in managing this impending crush syndrome?

586. 
You are pinned down by hostile fire. A casualty sustains a massive junctional hemorrhage that is successfully controlled with a hemostatic dressing. D.

587.

621.

A.

B.

C.

D. 
Immediately switch all intravenous fluids to 6% Hetastarch to aggressively pull the third-spaced fluid back into the vascular container.
622. 
You are managing a casualty in an austere, snow-covered environment (ambient temperature 25°F). 641.

D.

649.

C.

D.

Surgical exploration reveals that the liver is entirely pulverized, despite the bullet exiting intact without fragmenting. Conversely, a similar round striking the lung causes a clean, narrow tissue track. According to the physics of terminal ballistics, why is the temporary cavity catastrophically destructive to the liver but less so to the lungs?
A. 
The liver contains massive amounts of trace copper, which chemically reacts with the bullet jacket to form a localized explosion.
B. 
The lungs contain massive amounts of fluid that instantly cool the super-heated projectile, preventing thermal damage.
C. 
Lung tissue is highly elastic and possesses low specific gravity (air-filleD., allowing it to flexibly absorb and rebound from the temporary stretch, whereas the liver is dense, fluid-filled, and highly inelastic, resulting in catastrophic radial shattering when stretched beyond its tensile limits.
D. 
The bullet's vacuum pulls external air into the wound track, which physically buffers the lung tissue but violently combusts inside the liver.

689.

You must tag the child immediately as Expectant (Black) and move on, as resources cannot be wasted on apneic patients regardless of age.

D. 
You must administer 1 mg of intramuscular Epinephrine via auto-injector to chemically stimulate the central respiratory drive.
690. 
A casualty has bilateral traumatic mid-thigh amputations from an IED blast. Tourniquets are secured. Their blood pressure is 75/40 mmHg, and they concurrently have a severe closed Traumatic Brain Injury (GCS 6). They are conscious and screaming in agonizing pain. Why is Ketamine the absolute pharmacological standard of care in this specific, highly complex polytrauma scenario, as opposed to Oral Transmucosal Fentanyl Citrate (OTFC.?
A. 
Ketamine physically stimulates massive platelet aggregation, directly helping to form a stable clot at the amputation site.
B. 
Ketamine provides profound dissociative analgesia while preserving spontaneous respiratory drive and leveraging sympathetic stimulation to support, or slightly elevate, the profoundly depressed blood pressure, which is absolutely critical to maintaining Cerebral Perfusion Pressure (CPP) for the injured brain.
C. 
OTFC chemically neutralizes the Tranexamic Acid (TXA. required for hemorrhage control, while Ketamine enhances it.
D. 
Ketamine chemically reverses brainstem herniation by osmotically drawing fluid out of the cranial vault.
691. 
You are managing a casualty extricated from a collapsed structure after 18 hours. They present with profound crush syndrome, severe lactic acidosis, and wide QRS complexes on the ECG. You must immediately administer intravenous Calcium to stabilize the myocardial cell membranes. You have both Calcium Gluconate and Calcium Chloride in your austere kit. If you elect to use Calcium Chloride, what critical pharmacological danger must you meticulously manage?
A. 
Calcium Chloride violently neutralizes the oxygen-carrying capacity of whole blood; it must only be given after the massive transfusion is complete.
B. 
Calcium Chloride strictly requires mixing with Sodium Bicarbonate prior to infusion to chemically activate.
C. 
Calcium Chloride provides three times the elemental calcium of Gluconate, but it causes catastrophic, severe tissue necrosis if it extravasates (leaks) into the surrounding tissue; it is strictly preferred via a central line, requiring meticulous, continuous verification if given via a peripheral IV or IO.
D. 
Calcium Chloride triggers immediate, massive systemic vasodilation that will completely crash the patient's blood pressure.

696.

697. 
You are pinned down by hostile fire. A casualty sustains a massive internal truncal hemorrhage. Due to the tactical situation, extraction is severely delayed. A.

C. 
TXA causes immediate, massive systemic vasodilation if given past 3 hours, completely crashing the patient's remaining blood pressure.
D. 
TXA requires immediate refrigeration after 3 hours of ambient field exposure; administering warm TXA causes profound anaphylaxis.

You are initiating fluid resuscitation. Why is whole blood heavily prioritized over standard crystalloids in veterinary tactical medicine for severe trauma?
A. 
Canines lack a sufficient volume of circulating plasma; administering crystalloids instantly triggers fatal, dilutional hyponatremia.
B. 
Crystalloids chemically interact with the K9's undercoat, triggering immediate malignant hyperthermia.
C. 
Canines are incredibly susceptible to acute hemodilution; excessive clear fluids will rapidly dilute their naturally robust coagulation factors, instantly "popping the clot" and leading to fatal exsanguination, while whole blood restores both volume and critical clotting factors.
D. 
Intravenous crystalloids are physically impossible to administer to a canine in the field without utilizing a specialized, surgically implanted central venous catheter.
724. 
You are providing prolonged field care (72 hours) in a highly austere environment. A casualty sustained a gunshot wound to the abdomen with significant bowel evisceration. C. 
Amoxicillin/Clavulanate 875 mg orally twice daily.
D. 
Moxifloxacin 400 mg orally or intravenously once daily.
725. 
An entry team breaches an illicit chemical processing facility. An operator is exposed to a dense, heavier-than-air gas smelling distinctly of newly mown hay. However, 6 hours later, the operator develops severe, intractable coughing, frothy pink sputum, and profound, rapid-onset hypoxia (SpO2 78%). A.

B. 
Strict rest, supplemental high-flow oxygen, and the potential application of continuous positive airway pressure (CPAP) to forcefully keep the damaged, fluid-filled alveoli open.

D.

726. 
You are pinned down by hostile fire. A casualty sustains a massive internal truncal hemorrhage. Extraction is severely delayed. Exactly 4.5 hours after the precise time of injury, extraction is achieved, and you establish intravenous access. According to strict CoTCCC guidelines, why must Tranexamic Acid (TXA. be entirely withheld at this specific moment?
A. 
TXA violently crystallizes when exposed to the lactic acid built up over 4.5 hours of shock, causing fatal micro-emboli.
B. 
Administering TXA past the 3-hour mark paradoxically increases mortality by actively triggering Disseminated Intravascular Coagulation (DIC. and fatal thromboembolic events, as the body's natural coagulation cascade shifts from a bleeding phase into a pro-thrombotic phase.
C. 
TXA causes immediate, massive systemic vasodilation if given past 3 hours, completely crashing the patient's remaining blood pressure.
D. 
TXA requires immediate refrigeration after 3 hours of ambient field exposure; administering warm TXA causes profound anaphylaxis.
727. 
An operator is extricated after being pinned beneath a collapsed concrete wall for 16 hours. They present with profound crush syndrome, severe lactic acidosis, and wide QRS complexes on the ECG. You must immediately administer intravenous Calcium to stabilize the myocardial cell membranes. You have both Calcium Gluconate and Calcium Chloride in your austere kit. Why is Calcium Gluconate generally preferred over Calcium Chloride for peripheral intravenous administration in the field?
A. 
Calcium Chloride violently neutralizes the oxygen-carrying capacity of whole blood; it must only be given after the massive transfusion is complete.
B. 
Calcium Chloride triggers immediate, massive systemic vasodilation that will completely crash the patient's blood pressure.
C. 
Calcium Chloride provides significantly more elemental calcium, but it acts as a severe, catastrophic vesicant; if the peripheral IV line infiltrates, it will cause massive, irreversible local tissue necrosis and sloughing, whereas Gluconate is vastly safer for peripheral veins.
D. 
Calcium Chloride strictly requires mixing with Sodium Bicarbonate prior to infusion to chemically activate.
728. 
A hostile combatant is struck in the right upper quadrant of the abdomen by a high-velocity 5.56x45mm NATO projectile. Surgical exploration reveals that the liver is entirely pulverized. According to the physics of terminal ballistics, why does the temporary cavity cause such catastrophic, disproportionate destruction to solid organs like the liver or spleen, as opposed to the lungs?
A. 
The liver is dense, fluid-filled, and highly inelastic; it cannot stretch to accommodate the massive kinetic energy transfer of the temporary cavity, resulting in catastrophic radial fracturing and complete organ disintegration.
B. 
The liver contains massive amounts of trace copper, which chemically reacts with the bullet jacket to form a localized explosion.
C. 
The bullet's vacuum pulls external air into the wound track, which physically buffers the lung tissue but violently combusts inside the liver.
D. 
The extreme hydrostatic pressure wave pushes hepatic blood retrograde into the heart, causing secondary rupture.
729. 
You are managing an operator with massive bilateral lower extremity amputations in a temperate environment (ambient temperature 60°F). Despite the moderate temperature, you aggressively wrap the casualty in an active heating blanket (e.g., Ready-Heat). What is the precise, molecular mechanism by which you are directly treating their hemorrhage control?
A. 
The heat directly triggers massive, spontaneous platelet aggregation at the amputation sites.
B. 
By preventing hypothermia, you are actively preserving the function of the highly temperature-dependent serine protease enzymes in the coagulation cascade, allowing stable fibrin cross-linking to occur; hypothermia physically paralyzes these enzymes regardless of how much blood or TXA is administered.
C. 
The active warming blanket serves strictly as a psychological "grounding" mechanism to prevent the onset of acute combat stress reactions.
D. 
The heat causes massive systemic vasodilation, which lowers the patient's blood pressure and slows the arterial bleeding.
730.

731.

A. 
You perform absolutely no advanced airway management during Care Under Fire; you must return suppressive fire, direct the casualty to move to cover and assume a position that passively protects their airway (e.g., leaning forwarD., and strictly defer all surgical interventions until you have reached the warm zone.
B. 
Immediately break cover, sprint to the casualty, and perform a rapid surgical cricothyroidotomy, as airway loss is the absolute fastest cause of preventable death.

Throw a chemical smoke grenade, sprint to the casualty, and perform a blind digital intubation before retreating.

732. 
An operator breaches an illicit chemical processing facility and is exposed to heavy concentrations of Hydrogen Cyanide (AC. gas. They collapse in a seizure. What is the clinical significance of this profound color change?
A. 
It indicates a massive, fatal allergic reaction to the antidote; you must immediately administer high-dose Epinephrine and Corticosteroids.
B. 
It proves the chemical exposure was actually a blister agent (like Sulfur MustarD., not Cyanide, and you have administered the incorrect antidote.
C. 
It indicates that the cyanide has successfully paralyzed the red blood cells, and the patient requires an immediate, massive whole blood transfusion.
D.

733. 
You are 24 hours into a prolonged field care scenario treating an 85kg operator with 45% TBSA partial and full-thickness burns resulting from an IED blast. You have been aggressively utilizing the CoTCCC Rule of 10s fluid resuscitation protocol. The operator's urine output suddenly drops to 5 mL/hr, and the urine turns the color of dark tea. The patient is exhibiting signs of severe rhabdomyolysis. What is the doctrinally correct, immediate adjustment to the austere fluid resuscitation strategy?
A. 
Decrease the intravenous fluid rate by 50% immediately, as the anuria indicates the patient has entered irreversible acute renal failure and volume overload will cause fatal pulmonary edema.
B. 
Increase the fluid rate significantly (often targeting a highly elevated urine output of 75-100 mL/hr) to aggressively flush the kidneys and prevent toxic myoglobin precipitation in the renal tubules, and strongly consider administering Sodium Bicarbonate.
C. 
Administer massive doses of intravenous loop diuretics (e.g., Furosemide) to mechanically force the kidneys to produce urine without increasing the systemic fluid volume.
D. 
Immediately switch all intravenous fluids to 6% Hetastarch to aggressively pull the third-spaced fluid back into the vascular container.
734. 
A federal tracking team is operating at 14,000 feet in the Rocky Mountains. D.

735.

C.

D.

736. 
A casualty has bilateral traumatic amputations and is in severe hemorrhagic shock. You are executing a massive transfusion protocol utilizing 1:1:1 component therapy (Packed Red Blood Cells, Fresh Frozen Plasma, and Platelets). Why is it strictly doctrinally required to resuscitate using this balanced ratio rather than utilizing PRBCs and standard crystalloids alone?
A. 
The 1:1:1 ratio is strictly necessary because FFP contains the concentrated calcium required to initiate the electrical impulses of the heart during shock.
B. 
Resuscitating with PRBCs and crystalloids alone inherently causes severe dilutional coagulopathy; balancing with FFP and Platelets accurately replaces the consumed clotting factors necessary to actually halt the ongoing internal hemorrhage.
C. 
PRBCs alone are heavily hyperosmotic and will immediately trigger fatal, flash pulmonary edema.
D. 
PRBCs and crystalloids cause instant, fatal citrate toxicity; balancing with FFP and platelets chemically neutralizes the citrate preservative.

D.

738.

C.

D. 
It is a strict legal requirement mandated by the Federal Tort Claims Act to prevent animal cruelty lawsuits.
